Step 16: Constructing the skeleton.

As in Step 14 of the proof of
Theorem

8.1
, we now define the skeleton
\mathcal{L}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
for the almost spanning cycle.
We again let
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\coloneqq\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}
.
The skeleton
\mathcal{L}
again satisfies

(

⁢
S
1

)

–

(

⁢
S
7

)

in the proof of
Theorem

8.1
, except that
Q^{\prime}
now plays the role of
G^{\prime}
and,

(

⁢
S
3

)

and

(

⁢
S
7

)

are now replaced by the following.

(\mathrm{S}3)

For every
k\in[r-1]
, if
x_{k}
and
x_{k+1}
do not both lie in the same slice of a cube molecule represented in
\tau_{0}^{\prime}
, then
\{x_{k},x_{k+1}\}\in E(Q^{\prime})
.
Moreover, in this case, if
x_{k+1}
lies in a cube molecule represented in
\tau_{0}^{\prime}
, then
x_{k+2}
lies in the same slice of this cube molecule as
x_{k+1}
.

(\mathrm{S}7)

\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\cap(\mathfrak{L}\cup\mathfrak{R}_{1}\cup V^{\mathrm{abs}}\cup V(\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{CS}}))=\varnothing
and
L^{\bullet}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
.

The construction of
\mathcal{L}
is identical to that in
Theorem

8.1
.
The only difference is that the ‘forbidden set
\mathcal{F}
’ which the skeleton has to avoid is replaced by
\mathcal{F}\coloneqq\mathfrak{L}\cup\mathfrak{R}_{1}\cup L^{\bullet}\cup V(\mathcal{M}_{\mathit{CS}})
(this is required to ensure that

(

⁢
S
7

)

holds).
For each
i\in[m^{\prime}]_{0}
such that
v_{i}
is an atomic vertex and each
j\in[t]
, let
\mathfrak{J}_{i,j}\coloneqq\{k\in[r]:x_{k},x_{k+1}\in V(\mathcal{M}_{j}(v_{i}))\}
and
S_{i,j}\coloneqq\{\{x_{k},x_{k+1}\}:k\in\mathfrak{J}_{i,j}\}
.

Step 17. Incorporating special absorbing structures into the skeleton.

In this step, we are going to incorporate all special absorbing structures fixed in Step 11 into the skeleton we just constructed.
Note that all cube molecules referred to are represented by atomic vertices in
\tau^{\prime}_{0}
, and all slices referred to are one of the
t
slices of each of these molecules defined in Step 3.
For each
x\in\mathcal{U}
, consider the consistent system of paths
\mathit{CS}(x)
and the special absorbing structure
\mathit{SA}(x)
given by

(

⁢

C
′

7

)

.
By

(

⁢
S
7

)

, we have that
\mathcal{L}
avoids
\mathit{CS}(x)
.
Moreover, by the definition of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3}
at the end of Step 11, each
C^{\prime}\in\mathbf{C}(\mathit{SA}(x))
is a clone of some
C\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3}
.
Thus, by

(

⁢
S
5

)

we have that
\mathcal{L}
has positive intersection with each slice which contains a vertex of
\mathrm{end}(\mathit{CS}(x))
.

Recall that a special absorbing structure is a tuple of paths and cubes (see
Section

9.1
).
For each
z\in\mathcal{U}
, let
P^{z}_{1}
denote the first path of
\mathit{SA}(z)
.
Let
x(z)\in\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
be the first vertex in
\mathcal{L}
that is contained in the slice which contains the first vertex of
P^{z}_{1}
.
Let
x^{\prime}(z)
be the successor of
x(z)
in
\mathcal{L}
(in particular, by

(

⁢
S
3

)

, both
x(z)
and
x^{\prime}(z)
lie in the same slice).
Now, for each
z\in\mathcal{U}
, depending on the type of the special absorbing structure
\mathit{SA}(z)
, we will update
\mathcal{L}
in different ways.

(I)

If
\mathit{SA}(z)
is a special absorbing structure of Type I, proceed as follows.
Let
P^{z}_{1},\dots,P^{z}_{6}
be the six paths of
\mathit{SA}(z)
.
Let
S\coloneqq\bigtimes_{i=1}^{6}P^{z}_{i}
and let
S^{-1}
denote the same sequence of vertices in reverse order.
If
x(z)
has opposite parity to the initial vertex of
P^{z}_{1}
, then we replace the segment
(x(z),x^{\prime}(z))
of
\mathcal{L}
by
(x(z),S,x^{\prime}(z))
; otherwise, we we replace the segment
(x(z),x^{\prime}(z))
by
(x(z),S^{-1},x^{\prime}(z))
.

(II)

If
\mathit{SA}(z)
is a special absorbing structure of Type II, we proceed as follows.
Let
P^{z}_{1}
and
P^{z}_{2}
be the two paths of
\mathit{SA}(z)
.
Let
y^{1}
and
x^{1}_{0}
be the first and last vertices of
P^{z}_{1}
, and let
y^{2}
and
x^{2}_{0}
be the first and last vertices of
P^{z}_{2}
, respectively.
Let
v,v^{\prime}\in V(\tau_{0}^{\prime})
and
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\in[t]
be such that
y^{1}\in V(\mathcal{M}_{t_{1}}(v))
,
x^{1}_{0}\in V(\mathcal{M}_{t_{2}}(v^{\prime}))
, and
y^{2}\in V(\mathcal{M}_{t_{3}}(v^{\prime}))
(this implies
x_{0}^{2}\in V(\mathcal{M}_{t_{3}}(v))
).

We now define two sequences of vertices
S^{z}_{1}
and
S^{z}_{2}
following similar ideas to Step 16.
Recall that, for each
i\in[2^{s}]
, we use
\hat{e}_{i}
to denote the direction of the edges between
L_{i}
and
L_{i+1}
.
If
t_{3}\geq t_{2}
, let
m_{1}\coloneqq t_{3}-t_{2}
; otherwise, let
m_{1}\coloneqq t-(t_{2}-t_{3})
.
For each
k\in[m_{1}-1]_{0}
, iteratively choose a vertex
y^{1}_{k}\in V(\mathcal{A}_{(t_{2}+k)q}(v^{\prime}))\setminus\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
satisfying that

1
.

y_{k}^{1}\neq_{\mathrm{p}}x_{k}^{1}
;

2
.

x_{k+1}^{1}\coloneqq y_{k}^{1}+\hat{e}_{(t_{2}+k)q}\notin\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
, and

3
.

\{y_{k}^{1},x_{k+1}^{1}\}\in E(Q^{\prime})
.

We set
S^{z}_{1}\coloneqq\bigtimes_{k=0}^{m_{1}-1}(y_{k}^{1},x_{k+1}^{1})
.

In order to construct
S_{2}^{z}
, we proceed similarly.
If
t_{3}\geq t_{1}
, let
m_{2}\coloneqq t_{3}-t_{1}
; otherwise, let
m_{2}\coloneqq t-(t_{1}-t_{3})
.
For each
k\in[m_{2}-1]_{0}
, iteratively choose a vertex
y^{2}_{k}\in V(\mathcal{A}_{(t_{3}-k-1)q+1}(v))\setminus\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
satisfying that

1
.

y_{k}^{2}\neq_{\mathrm{p}}x_{k}^{2}
;

2
.

x_{k+1}^{2}\coloneqq y_{k}^{2}+\hat{e}_{(t_{3}-k-1)q}\notin\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
, and

3
.

\{y_{k}^{2},x_{k+1}^{2}\}\in E(Q^{\prime})
.

Now, let
S^{z}_{2}\coloneqq\bigtimes_{k=0}^{m_{2}-1}(y_{k}^{2},x_{k+1}^{2})
.

Let
S\coloneqq P^{z}_{1}\times S^{z}_{1}\times P^{z}_{2}\times S^{z}_{2}
, and let
S^{-1}
denote the same sequence of vertices in reverse order.
Finally, we replace the segment
(x(z),x^{\prime}(z))
of
\mathcal{L}
by
(x(z),S,x^{\prime}(z))
if
x(z)
has parity opposite to the initial vertex of
P^{z}_{1}
; otherwise, we replace
(x(z),x^{\prime}(z))
by
(x(z),S^{-1},x^{\prime}(z))
.

(III)

If
\mathit{SA}(z)
is a special absorbing structure of Type III, we proceed as follows.
Let
P^{z}_{1}
,
P^{z}_{2}
and
P^{z}_{3}
be the three paths of
\mathit{SA}(z)
.
For each
i\in[3]
, let
y^{i}
and
x_{0}^{i}
be the first and last vertices of
P^{z}_{i}
, respectively.
Let
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\in V(\tau_{0}^{\prime})
and
t_{1},t_{2}\in[t]
be such that
y^{1}\in V(\mathcal{M}_{t_{1}}(v_{1}))
,
y^{2}\in V(\mathcal{M}_{t_{1}}(v_{2}))
and
y^{3}\in V(\mathcal{M}_{t_{2}}(v_{3}))
(note this implies that
x^{1}_{0}\in V(\mathcal{M}_{t_{2}}(v_{2}))
,
x^{2}_{0}\in V(\mathcal{M}_{t_{1}}(v_{3}))
and
x^{3}_{0}\in V(\mathcal{M}_{t_{2}}(v_{1}))
).

For each
i\in[3]
, we define a sequence
S^{z}_{i}
as follows.
If
t_{2}\geq t_{1}
, let
m^{*}\coloneqq t_{2}-t_{1}
; otherwise, let
m^{*}\coloneqq t-(t_{1}-t_{2})
.
For each
k\in[m^{*}-1]_{0}
, iteratively choose three vertices
y^{1}_{k}\in V(\mathcal{A}_{(t_{2}-k-1)q+1}(v_{2}))\setminus\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
,
y^{2}_{k}\in V(\mathcal{A}_{(t_{1}+k)q}(v_{3}))\setminus\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
and
y^{3}_{k}\in V(\mathcal{A}_{(t_{2}-k-1)q+1}(v_{1}))\setminus\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
satisfying that

1
.

y_{k}^{i}\neq_{\mathrm{p}}x_{k}^{i}
for all
i\in[3]
;

2
.

x_{k+1}^{1}\coloneqq y_{k}^{1}+\hat{e}_{(t_{2}-k-1)q}\notin\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
,
x_{k+1}^{2}\coloneqq y_{k}^{2}+\hat{e}_{(t_{1}+k)q}\notin\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
and
x_{k+1}^{3}\coloneqq y_{k}^{3}+\hat{e}_{(t_{2}-k-1)q}\notin\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
, and

3
.

\{y_{k}^{i},x_{k+1}^{i}\}\in E(Q^{\prime})
for all
i\in[3]
.

Then, for each
i\in[3]
, we define
S^{z}_{i}\coloneqq\bigtimes_{k=0}^{m^{*}-1}(y_{k}^{i},x_{k+1}^{i})
.

Let
S\coloneqq\bigtimes_{i=1}^{3}(P^{z}_{i}\times S^{z}_{i})
, and let
S^{-1}
denote the same sequence in reverse order.
Finally, we replace the segment
(x(z),x^{\prime}(z))
of
\mathcal{L}
by
(x(z),S,x^{\prime}(z))
if
x(z)
has parity opposite to the initial vertex of
P^{z}_{1}
; otherwise, we replace
(x(z),x^{\prime}(z))
by
(x(z),S^{-1},x^{\prime}(z))
.

Write
\mathcal{L}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
, for some
r\in\mathbb{N}
, for the extended skeleton into which all the special absorbing structures
\mathit{SA}(z)
for
z\in\mathcal{U}
have been incorporated, and let
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\coloneqq\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}
.
It follows from

(

⁢
S
1

)

–

(

⁢
S
7

)

and the construction above (together with the choice of
v_{0}
in Step 12) that the following properties hold:

(\mathrm{S^{\prime}}1)

For all distinct
k,k^{\prime}\in[r]
, we have that
x_{k}\neq x_{k^{\prime}}
.

(\mathrm{S^{\prime}}2)

\{x_{1},x_{r}\}\in E(Q^{\prime})
.

(\mathrm{S^{\prime}}3)

For every
k\in[r-1]
, if
x_{k}
and
x_{k+1}
do not both lie in the same slice of a cube molecule represented in
\tau_{0}^{\prime}
, then
\{x_{k},x_{k+1}\}\in E((H\setminus F)\cup H^{\prime}\cup Q^{\prime})
.
Moreover, in this case
\{x_{k},x_{k+1}\}\in E(Q^{\prime})
unless both
x_{k}
and
x_{k+1}
lie in
\mathit{SA}^{v}
.

(\mathrm{S^{\prime}}4)

For every
i\in[m^{\prime}]_{0}
and every
j\in[t]
, no three consecutive vertices of
\mathcal{L}
lie in
\mathcal{M}_{j}(v_{i})
(here
\mathcal{L}
is viewed as a cyclic sequence of vertices).

(\mathrm{S^{\prime}}5)

For every
i\in[m^{\prime}]
such that
v_{i}
is an atomic vertex and every
j\in[t]
, we have that
|V(\mathcal{M}_{j}(v_{i}))\cap\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}|
is even and
4\leq|V(\mathcal{M}_{j}(v_{i}))\cap\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}|\leq 14
.
Moreover,
|V(\mathcal{M}_{t}(v_{0}))\cap\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}|=4
.

(\mathrm{S^{\prime}}6)

For all
k\in[r]
except two values, we have that
x_{k}\neq_{\mathrm{p}}x_{k+1}
.
The remaining two values
k_{1},k_{2}\in[r]
correspond to two pairs of vertices
x_{k_{1}},x_{k_{1}+1},x_{k_{2}},x_{k_{2}+1}\in V(\mathcal{M}_{t}(v_{0}))
.
For these two values, we have that
x_{k_{1}}\neq_{\mathrm{p}}x_{k_{2}}
and either

(\mathrm{i})

x_{k_{1}}=_{\mathrm{p}}x_{k_{1}+1}
and
x_{k_{2}}=_{\mathrm{p}}x_{k_{2}+1}
, or

(\mathrm{ii})

x_{k_{1}}\neq_{\mathrm{p}}x_{k_{1}+1}
and
x_{k_{2}}\neq_{\mathrm{p}}x_{k_{2}+1}
,

where
x_{k_{1}},x_{k_{2}}\in V(\mathcal{A}_{(t-1)q+1}(v_{0}))
and
x_{k_{1}+1},x_{k_{2}+1}\in V(\mathcal{A}_{tq}(v_{0}))
.

(\mathrm{S^{\prime}}7)

Step 19: Absorbing vertices to form a Hamilton cycle.

Similarly as in Step 16 of the proof of
Theorem

8.1
, for each

we now replace the edge

by the path

lies in

).
Clearly, this incorporates all vertices of

into the cycle and, by

(

⁢

2

)

and

(

⁢

3

)

Moreover, since by

(

3

)

, all these endvertices avoid

.
Thus, by

(

⁢
HC
4

)

, for each

the edges at

in

are still
\{x,x+a(x)\}
and

, and so, in particular, by

(

⁢

C
′

7

)

these edges belong to
H^{\prime}
.

It now remains to show that
\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}
is
(\mathcal{U},\ell^{2},s)
-good.
Fix any vertex

.
Let
Y_{x}\coloneqq N_{\mathcal{Q}^{n}}(x)\setminus(V(\mathit{SA}(x))\cup V^{\mathrm{abs}})
(that is, by

(

⁢

C
′

3

)

is the set of all vertices in

which lie in clones of cubes which are represented in
\tau_{0}^{\prime}
by atomic vertices).
By (
9.14
), we have that
|Y_{x}|\geq(1-2/\ell^{4})n-|V(\mathit{SA}(x))|\geq(1-1/\ell^{3})n
.

9.3

(
ii
)

implies that

, so by definition we have that

.

(

⁢

5

)

that, for each

incident to

lies in

.
But

is
(\mathcal{U},2\ell^{2},s)
-good by

.
Now, even if all the edges incident to the remaining vertices

used the same pair of directions, it follows that the edges of
\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}
incident to the vertices in

use each direction of
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
which is not an
s
-direction at most
n/\ell^{3}+n/(2\ell^{2})\leq n/\ell^{2}
times.
∎

9.4.
Proofs of
Theorem

1.8
and
Theorem

1.5

We now deduce
Theorems

1.8
and
1.5
from
Theorem

9.6
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.8
.

Let
0<1/n\ll 1/\ell\ll\varepsilon_{1}\ll\varepsilon\ll\varepsilon_{2}\ll\gamma\ll 1/k\leq 1
.
Let
s\coloneqq 10\ell
.
Let
H^{*}\sim\mathcal{Q}^{n}_{1/2-2\varepsilon}
and
Q\sim\mathcal{Q}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
.
Observe that
H^{*}\cup Q\sim\mathcal{Q}^{n}_{1/2-\varepsilon^{\prime}}
for some
\varepsilon^{\prime}\geq\varepsilon
, so it suffices to prove that
H\cup H^{*}\cup Q
contains the desired Hamilton cycles and perfect matchings.

By
Lemma

9.4
with
2\varepsilon
playing the role of
\varepsilon
, we have that a.a.s.
H^{*}
is
(s,\ell,\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\gamma,\mathcal{U}(H^{*},\varepsilon_{1}))
-robust.
Condition on this event and let
\mathcal{U}\coloneqq\mathcal{U}(H^{*},\varepsilon_{1})
.
By an application of
Lemma

9.4
(ii)
, it follows that there exists a decomposition of
H^{*}
into
r\coloneqq\lceil k/2\rceil
edge-disjoint spanning subgraphs
H^{*}_{1},\ldots,H^{*}_{r}
such that, for every
i\in[r]
, we have that
H^{*}_{i}
is
(s,\ell,\varepsilon_{1}/(2r),\varepsilon_{2},\gamma/r^{10},\mathcal{U})
-robust.

Consider a random decomposition of
Q
into
r
edge-disjoint spanning subgraphs
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{r}
in such a way that, if
e\in Q
, then
e
is assigned to one of the
Q_{i}
chosen uniformly at random and independently of all other edges.
It follows that, for all
i\in[r]
, we have
Q_{i}\sim\mathcal{Q}^{n}_{\varepsilon/r}
.

Let
\Phi
be a constant such that
Theorem

9.6
holds with
\varepsilon_{1}/(2r)
,
\gamma/r^{10}
,
\varepsilon/r
and
r+2
playing the roles of
\varepsilon_{1}
,
\gamma
,
\eta
and
c
, respectively.
(In particular,
\Phi\geq r
.)
For each
i\in[r]
, apply
Theorem

9.6
with
H_{i}^{*}
,
Q_{i}
,
\varepsilon_{1}/(2r)
,
\gamma/r^{10}
,
\varepsilon/r
and
r+2
playing the roles of
H
,
Q
,
\varepsilon_{1}
,
\gamma
,
\eta
and
c
, respectively, to conclude that a.a.s. there is a
(\mathcal{U},\ell^{2},s)
-good subgraph
Q_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq Q_{i}
with
\Delta(Q_{i}^{\prime})\leq\Phi
such that, for every
H^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}^{n}
such that
d_{H^{\prime}}(x)\geq 2
for every
x\in\mathcal{U}
, and every
F\subseteq\mathcal{Q}^{n}
with
\Delta(F)\leq(r+2)\Phi
which is
(\mathcal{U},\ell,s)
-good, we have that
((H^{*}_{i}\cup Q_{i})\setminus F)\cup H^{\prime}\cup Q_{i}^{\prime}
contains a
(\mathcal{U},\ell^{2},s)
-good Hamilton cycle
C
such that, for all
x\in\mathcal{U}
, both edges of
C
incident to
x
belong to
H^{\prime}
.
Condition on the event that this holds for all
i\in[r]
(which holds a.a.s. by a union bound).
Since the
Q_{i}
are pairwise edge-disjoint, so are the
Q_{i}^{\prime}
.

Now consider the graph
H
from the statement of
Theorem

1.8
.
By

(

⁢
R
3

)

in
Definition

9.3
, we can greedily find
r
edge-disjoint subgraphs
H_{1},\ldots,H_{r}\subseteq H
such that

(i)

for each
i\in[\lfloor k/2\rfloor]
, we have that
|E(H_{i})|=2|\mathcal{U}|
and
d_{H_{i}}(x)=2
for every
x\in\mathcal{U}
, and

(ii)

if
2r=k+1
, then
H_{r}
is a matching of size
|\mathcal{U}|
such that
d_{H_{r}}(x)=1
for all
x\in\mathcal{U}
.

Suppose first that
2r=k
.
We are going to find
r
edge-disjoint
(\mathcal{U},\ell^{2},s)
-good Hamilton cycles
C_{1},\ldots,C_{r}
with
H_{i}\subseteq C_{i}
iteratively.
Suppose that for some
i\in[r]
we have already found
C_{1},\ldots,C_{i-1}
.
Let
F_{i}\coloneqq\bigcup_{j=1}^{r}Q_{j}^{\prime}\cup\bigcup_{j=1}^{i-1}C_{j}
.
It follows by construction that
F_{i}
is
(\mathcal{U},\ell,s)
-good and
\Delta(F_{i})\leq r(\Phi+2)\leq(r+2)\Phi
.
Then, by the conditioning above, the graph
((H_{i}^{*}\cup Q_{i})\setminus F_{i})\cup H_{i}\cup Q_{i}^{\prime}
must contain a
(\mathcal{U},\ell^{2},s)
-good Hamilton cycle
C_{i}
such that, for each
u\in\mathcal{U}
, both edges of
C_{i}
incident to
x
belong to
H_{i}
.
In particular,
H_{i}\subseteq C_{i}
.
Take one such cycle and proceed.

In order to see that these
r
cycles are pairwise edge-disjoint, suppose that there exist
i,j\in[r]
with
i<j
such that
E(C_{i})\cap E(C_{j})\neq\varnothing
, and let
e\in E(C_{i})\cap E(C_{j})
.
Observe that
e\notin E(H_{i})\cup E(H_{j})
because, otherwise, we would have
e
incident to some vertex
x\in\mathcal{U}
, and we know that both edges incident to
x
in
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
belong to
H_{i}
and
H_{j}
, respectively, which are edge-disjoint.
Therefore, since
e\in E(C_{i})
and
Q_{j}^{\prime}\subseteq F_{i}\setminus Q_{i}^{\prime}
, we must have that
e\notin E(Q_{j}^{\prime})
.
However, since
e\in E(C_{j})
and
e\in E(F_{j})
by definition, we must have
e\in E(Q_{j}^{\prime})
, a contradiction.

Suppose now that
2r=k+1
.
Let
F_{1}\coloneqq\bigcup_{j=1}^{r}Q_{j}^{\prime}
, so
\Delta(F_{1})\leq r\Phi
and it is
(\mathcal{U},\ell,s)
-good.
By the conditioning above,
((H^{*}_{1}\cup Q_{1})\setminus F_{1})\cup H_{1}\cup Q_{1}^{\prime}
contains a
(\mathcal{U},\ell^{2},s)
-good Hamilton cycle
C
with
H_{1}\subseteq C
.
We split
C
into two perfect matchings
M_{1}\cup M_{2}
(observe that both of them are
(\mathcal{U},\ell^{2},s)
-good) and redefine
H_{r}\coloneqq H_{r}\cup\{e\in M_{2}:\mathcal{U}\cap e\neq\varnothing\}
, so that
H_{r}
now satisfies
(i)
.
Now, for each
i\in\{2,\ldots,r\}
, we proceed as follows.
Let
F_{i}\coloneqq M_{1}\cup\bigcup_{j=1}^{r}Q_{j}^{\prime}\cup\bigcup_{j=2}^{i-1}C_{j}
.
It follows by construction that
F_{i}
is
(\mathcal{U},\ell,s)
-good and
\Delta(F_{i})\leq r(\Phi+2)\leq(r+2)\Phi
.
Then, by the conditioning above, the graph
((H_{i}^{*}\cup Q_{i})\setminus F_{i})\cup H_{i}\cup Q_{i}^{\prime}
must contain a
(\mathcal{U},\ell^{2},s)
-good Hamilton cycle
C_{i}
with
H_{i}\subseteq C_{i}
.
Take one such cycle and proceed.
The fact that the graphs
M_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{r}
are pairwise edge-disjoint can be proved as in the previous case.
∎

We now prove
Theorem

1.5
.
Recall from
Section

1.4
that, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any graph
G\subseteq\mathcal{Q}^{n}
, we say that
G\in\bm{\delta}k
if
\delta(G)\geq k
, and
G\in\mathcal{HM}k
if it contains
\lfloor k/2\rfloor
edge-disjoint Hamilton cycles and
k-2\lfloor k/2\rfloor
perfect matchings which are edge-disjoint from these cycles.
We say that
G\in\mathcal{P}k
if, for every spanning subgraph
H\subseteq\mathcal{Q}^{n}
with
H\in\bm{\delta}k
, we have
G\cup H\in\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}k
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.5
.

The case
k=1
of the statement was proved by
Bollobás 1990
, so we may assume
k\geq 2
.
Let
0<\varepsilon\ll 1/k
and
G\sim\mathcal{Q}^{n}_{1/2-\varepsilon}
.
By
Theorem

1.8
, we have
\mathbb{P}[G\in\mathcal{P}k]=1-o(1)
.
Also note that, by
Lemma

4.2
, we have that
\mathbb{P}[e(G)\geq(1/2-\varepsilon/2)n2^{n-1}]=o(1)
.
Hence,

\mathbb{P}[\{G\in\mathcal{P}k\}\wedge\{e(G)<(1/2-\varepsilon/2)n2^{n-1}\}]=1-o(1).

Thus, by a simple conditioning argument, there exists a positive integer
m<(1/2-\varepsilon/2)n2^{n-1}
such that

(9.16)

\mathbb{P}[G\in\mathcal{P}k\mid e(G)=m]=1-o(1).

Let
G_{m}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}^{n}
be a uniformly random subgraph of
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
with exactly
m
edges.
Since
\mathbb{P}[G\in\mathcal{P}k\mid e(G)=m]=\mathbb{P}[G_{m}\in\mathcal{P}k]
, by (
9.16
) we have

\mathbb{P}[G_{m}\in\mathcal{P}k]=1-o(1)
.
Now, condition on the event that
G_{m}\in\mathcal{P}k
and
\tau_{\bm{\delta}k}(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}^{n}}(\sigma))\geq(1/2-\varepsilon/4)n2^{n-1}
, which holds a.a.s.
Then, let
H\coloneqq\mathcal{Q}^{n}_{\tau_{\bm{\delta}k}(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}^{n}}(\sigma))}(\sigma)
.
It follows that
\delta(H)=k
and, by the definition of property
\mathcal{P}k
, we have that
H=G_{m}\cup H\in\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}k
, so
\tau_{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}k}(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}^{n}}(\sigma))\leq\tau_{\bm{\delta}k}(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}^{n}}(\sigma))
, as required.
∎

Appendix A
Proof of
Lemma

8.9

Proof of
Lemma

8.9
.

The proof is similar (but easier) to that of
Lemma

8.8
.
By relabelling the atoms, we may assume that
\mathcal{M^{*}}=\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\mathcal{A}_{t}
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that, for each
r\in[2]
, if
u_{r}\in R
, then
u_{r}=z_{1}
, and if
v_{r}\in R
, then
v_{r}=w_{t}
.
Moreover, we may assume that
x\neq_{\mathrm{p}}u_{1}
.
Let
S\coloneqq\{u_{1},v_{1},u_{2},v_{2}\}
.
Let
I_{R}\coloneqq\{k\in[t]:R\cap V(\mathcal{A}_{k})\cap S\neq\varnothing\}
,
R^{*}\coloneqq R\setminus\bigcup_{k\in I_{R}}V(\mathcal{A}_{k})
and
I_{R^{*}}\coloneqq\{k\in[t]:R^{*}\cap V(\mathcal{A}_{k})\neq\varnothing\}
.
For each
r\in[2]
, let
I_{R}^{r}\subseteq\{1,t\}
be such that
1\in I_{R}^{r}
if and only if
u_{r}\in R
and
t\in I_{R}^{r}
if and only if
v_{r}\in R
.
Note that
I_{R}=I_{R}^{1}\cup I_{R}^{2}
.
Fix an index
t^{*}\in[t-1]\setminus(I_{R^{*}}\cup\{1\})
.
If
|L|=2
, let
I_{L}^{1}\coloneqq\{i\}
,
I_{L}^{2}\coloneqq\{j\}
and
I_{L}\coloneqq\{i,j\}
; otherwise, let
I_{L}^{1}\coloneqq I_{L}^{2}\coloneqq I_{L}\coloneqq\{t^{*}\}
.

For each
r\in[2]
, we create an ordered list
\mathcal{L}_{r}
of vertices, which will be used to construct the vertex-disjoint paths
\mathcal{P}_{r}
.
Given any list of vertices
\mathcal{L}_{r}
, we write
L^{*}_{r}
to denote the (unordered) set of vertices in
\mathcal{L}_{r}
, and whenever
\mathcal{L}_{r}
is updated, we implicitly update
L_{r}^{*}
.
In the end, for each
r\in[2]
we will have a list of vertices
\mathcal{L}_{r}=(x_{1}^{r},\dots,x_{\ell_{r}}^{r})
.
For each
r\in[2]
and
k\in[t]
, let
I_{r}(k)\coloneqq\{h\in[\ell_{r}-1]:2\nmid h\text{ and }x^{r}_{h},x^{r}_{h+1}\in V(\mathcal{A}_{k})\}
.
We will require
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
to be vertex-disjoint and to satisfy the following properties:

(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}1)

\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are even.

(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}2)

For each
r\in[2]
, for all
h\in[\ell_{r}-1]
, if
h
is odd, then
x^{r}_{h},x^{r}_{h+1}\in V(\mathcal{A}_{k})
, for some
k\in[t]
; if
h
is even, then
x^{r}_{h}x^{r}_{h+1}\in E(G\cup\mathcal{M}^{*})
.

(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}3)

For all
k\in[t]
we have that
|I_{1}(k)|,|I_{2}(k)|\geq 1
and
2\leq|I_{1}(k)|+|I_{2}(k)|\leq 3
.

(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}4)

For each
r\in[2]
, the following holds.
For each
k\in[t]\setminus(I_{L}^{r}\cup I_{R}^{r})
and each
h\in I_{r}(k)
, we have
x_{h}^{r}\neq_{\mathrm{p}}x_{h+1}^{r}
.
For each
k\in I_{L}^{r}\cup I_{R}^{r}
, we have that
|I_{r}(k)|=1
and for the unique index
h\in I_{r}(k)
we have
x_{h}^{r}=_{\mathrm{p}}x_{h+1}^{r}
, with the same parity as
u_{r}
in the case when
k\in I_{L}^{r}
, and with parity opposite to that of the unique vertex in
\{w_{k},z_{k}\}\cap\{u_{r},v_{r}\}
in the case when
k\in I_{R}^{r}
.

(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}5)

For each
r\in[2]
, we have the following.
If
u_{r}\notin R
, then
u_{r}=x_{1}^{r}
.
If
v_{r}\notin R
, then
v_{r}=x^{r}_{\ell_{r}}
.
If
u_{r}\in R
(and thus
u_{r}=z_{1}
), then
w_{1}=x_{1}^{r}
and
u_{r}\notin L^{*}_{1}\cup L^{*}_{2}
.
If
v_{r}\in R
(and thus
v_{r}=w_{t}
), then
z_{t}=x_{\ell_{r}}^{r}
and
v_{r}\notin L^{*}_{1}\cup L^{*}_{2}
.

(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}6)

Every pair
(w_{k},z_{k})
with
\{w_{k},z_{k}\}\subseteq R^{*}
is contained in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
z_{k}
directly succeeds
w_{k}
or vice versa.

If
R^{*}\cap V(\mathcal{A}_{1})=\{w_{1},z_{1}\}
, then let
\mathcal{L}_{1}\coloneqq(u_{1},w_{1},z_{1})
, where we assume that
w_{1}\neq_{\mathrm{p}}u_{1}
; otherwise, let
\mathcal{L}_{1}\coloneqq(u_{1})
.
Observe once more that, in what follows, the existence of each alternating parity sequence follows from the bondedness of
\mathcal{M}
.

Let
F_{1}\coloneqq L\cup R\cup S
and let
t^{\bullet}_{1}\in I_{L}^{1}
.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{1}
be a
(u_{1},t^{\bullet}_{1},F_{1},R)
-alternating parity sequence.
If
u_{1}\in R
, update
\mathcal{L}_{1}\coloneqq\mathcal{S}_{1}
; otherwise, update
\mathcal{L}_{1}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{S}_{1}^{-}
.
Choose any vertex
u_{t^{\bullet}_{1}}\in V(\mathcal{A}_{t^{\bullet}_{1}})
with
u_{t^{\bullet}_{1}}\neq_{\mathrm{p}}u_{1}
, and let
\mathcal{S}_{2}
be a
(u_{t^{\bullet}_{1}},t,F_{1},R^{*})
-alternating parity sequence.
Update
\mathcal{L}_{1}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{S}_{2}^{-}
.
If
v_{1}\in R
, update
\mathcal{L}_{1}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}_{1}(z_{t})
.
Otherwise, update
\mathcal{L}_{1}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}_{1}(v_{1})
.

Next, let
F_{2}\coloneqq F_{1}\cup L_{1}^{*}
and let
t^{\bullet}_{2}\in I_{L}^{2}
.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{3}
be a
(u_{2},t^{\bullet}_{2},F_{2},R\cap V(\mathcal{A}_{1}))
-alternating parity sequence, and let
\mathcal{L}_{2}\coloneqq\mathcal{S}_{3}
.
Choose any vertex
u^{\prime}_{t^{\bullet}_{2}}\in V(\mathcal{A}_{t^{\bullet}_{2}})
with
u^{\prime}_{t^{\bullet}_{2}}\neq_{\mathrm{p}}u_{2}
, and let
\mathcal{S}_{4}
be a
(u^{\prime}_{t^{\bullet}_{2}},t,F_{2},\varnothing)
-alternating parity sequence.
Update
\mathcal{L}_{2}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}_{2}\mathcal{S}_{4}^{-}
.
Finally, if
v_{2}\in R
, update
\mathcal{L}_{2}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}_{2}(z_{t})
.
Otherwise, update
\mathcal{L}_{2}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}_{2}(v_{2})
.

Observe that
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
satisfy

(

⁢

L
′

1

)

–

(

⁢

L
′

6

)

.
We are now in a position to apply
Lemma

8.6
.
For each
k\in[t]
, let
t_{k}\coloneqq|I_{1}(k)|+|I_{2}(k)|
.
Again, for any
r\in[2]
and
k\in[t]
, for each
h\in I_{r}(k)
, we refer to the pair
x^{r}_{h},x^{r}_{h+1}
as a
\emph{matchablepair}
.
By

(

⁢

L
′

3

)

,

(

⁢

L
′

4

)

and
Lemma

8.6

(
i
)

, each
\mathcal{A}_{k}
with
k\in[t]\setminus(I_{L}\cup I_{R})
can be covered by
t_{k}
vertex-disjoint paths, each of whose endpoints are a matchable pair contained in
\mathcal{A}_{k}
.
Similarly, by

(

⁢

L
′

3

)

,

(

⁢

L
′

4

)

and
Lemma

8.6

(
ii
)

, each
\mathcal{A}_{k}
with
k\in I_{R}
contains
t_{k}
vertex-disjoint paths, each of whose endpoints are a matchable pair in
\mathcal{A}_{k}
, such that the union of these
t_{k}
paths covers precisely
V(\mathcal{A}_{k})\setminus(S\cap R)
.
Similarly, if
L\neq\varnothing
and
k\in I_{L}
, then
\mathcal{A}_{k}
contains
t_{k}
paths, each of whose endpoints are a matchable pair in
\mathcal{A}_{k}
, such that the union of these
t_{k}
paths covers precisely
V(\mathcal{A}_{k})\setminus L
.
Finally, by

(

⁢

L
′

3

)

,

(

⁢

L
′

4

)

and
Lemma

8.6

(
iii
)

, if
L=\varnothing
and
k\in I_{L}
(that is,
k=t^{*}
), then
\mathcal{A}_{k}
can be covered by
t_{k}
paths, each of whose endpoints are a matchable pair in
\mathcal{A}_{k}
.
For each matchable pair
x^{r}_{h},x^{r}_{h+1}
in
\mathcal{A}_{k}
, let us denote the corresponding path by
\mathcal{P}_{x^{r}_{h},x^{r}_{h+1}}
.

The paths
\mathcal{P}_{r}
required for
Lemma

8.9
can now be constructed as follows.
For each
r\in[2]
, let
\mathcal{P}_{r}
be the path obtained from the concatenation of the paths
\mathcal{P}_{x^{r}_{h},x^{r}_{h+1}}
, for each odd
h\in[\ell_{r}]
, via the edges
x^{r}_{h}x^{r}_{h+1}
for
h\in[\ell_{r}-1]
even.
By

(

⁢

L
′

5

)

, if
\mathcal{P}_{r}
does not contain
u_{r}
, then
\mathcal{P}_{r}
starts in
w_{1}
, and
u_{r}
does not lie in any other path; therefore, we can update
\mathcal{P}_{r}
as
\mathcal{P}_{r}\coloneqq u_{r}\mathcal{P}_{r}
.
Similarly, if
\mathcal{P}_{r}
does not contain
v_{r}
, then
\mathcal{P}_{r}
ends in
z_{t}
and
v_{r}
does not lie in any other path, hence we can update
\mathcal{P}_{r}
as
\mathcal{P}_{r}\coloneqq\mathcal{P}_{r}v_{r}
.
It follows that
V(\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2})=V(\mathcal{M}^{*})\setminus L
, and thus the paths
\mathcal{P}_{r}
are as required for
Lemma

8.9
.
∎
